[
20
]
and Ginzburg-Kazhdan
[
19
]
and borrows heavily from their ideas. Moreover, the foundational works of Achar
[
2
]
and Achar-Riche
[
3
]
were invaluable in preparing this work.

The author was supported by an NSF Graduate Research Fellowship during the writing of this paper.

2.
Automorphic Side

2.1.
Equivariant localization

We begin on the “geometric side” of Theorem
1.5.2
. Let
I\subseteq\check{\Delta}
denote a set of simple roots of
G
. Let
\lambda\in\Lambda^{+}
denote a dominant coweight and let
\mathrm{IC}_{\lambda}\in\mathcal{P}_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})
denote the corresponding irreducible perverse sheaf supported on
\mathrm{Gr}_{G}^{\leq\lambda}:=\overline{\mathrm{Gr}^{\lambda}_{G}}
, where
\mathrm{Gr}^{\lambda}_{G}=G(\mathcal{O})t^{\lambda}\subseteq\mathrm{Gr}_{G}
is the corresponding spherical orbit. Let
\mathrm{Gr}_{I}=\mathrm{Gr}_{L_{I}}
denote the affine Grassmannian of the Levi subgroup
L_{I}\subseteq G
and
i_{I}:\mathrm{Gr}_{I}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Gr}_{G}
the inclusion. We will study the cohomology
H^{*}_{L_{I}(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{I},i_{I}^{!}\mathrm{IC}_{\lambda})
through the technique of equivariant localization. We abuse notation and write
i_{I}^{!}\mathrm{IC}_{\lambda}\in D_{T}(\mathrm{Gr}_{L})
for the image of
i_{I}^{!}\mathrm{IC}_{\lambda}\in D_{L(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{L})
under the functor forgetting the
L(\mathcal{O})
-equivariance down to
T
-equivariance. Recall from
[
21
]
(especially the discussion surrounding
[
21
, Theorem 1.6.2]
) that a complex
F\in D_{T}(\mathrm{Gr}_{L})
is
equivariantly formal
if the spectral sequence

E_{2}^{p,q}=H_{T}^{p}(\mathrm{pt},H^{q}(\mathrm{Gr}_{I},F))\implies H^{p+q}_{T}(\mathrm{Gr}_{I},F)

degenerates at
E_{2}
.

Proposition 2.1.1
.

The complex
i_{I}^{!}\mathrm{IC}_{\lambda}\in D_{T}(\mathrm{Gr}_{L})
is equivariantly formal.

Proof.

For each point
x\in\mathrm{Gr}_{I}
, we have that
H^{j}(i_{x}^{!}i_{I}^{!}\mathrm{IC}_{\lambda})\simeq H^{j}(i_{x}^{!}\mathrm{IC}_{\lambda})
vanishes for
j\not\equiv\langle 2\check{\rho},\lambda\rangle
(mod 2), by the well-known parity vanishing property of
\mathrm{IC}_{\lambda}

[
5
, Lemma 4.5]
. Since
i^{!}\mathrm{IC}_{\lambda}
is constructible with respect to an affine paving of
\mathrm{Gr}_{I}
(the Schubert stratification by the orbits of an Iwahori subgroup
\mathcal{I}\subseteq L(\mathcal{O})
), a standard argument with the Cousin spectral sequence implies that
H^{j}(\mathrm{Gr}_{I},i_{I}^{!}\mathrm{IC}_{\lambda})
vanishes for
j\not\equiv\langle 2\check{\rho},\lambda\rangle
.

It is now clear from parity considerations that the spectral sequence

E_{2}^{p,q}=H^{p}_{T}(\mathrm{pt},H^{q}(\mathrm{Gr}_{I},i_{I}^{!}\mathrm{IC}_{\lambda}))\implies H^{p+q}_{T}(\mathrm{Gr}_{I},i_{I}^{!}\mathrm{IC}_{\lambda})

degenerates at
E_{2}
.
∎

Remark 2.1.2
.

Proposition
2.1.1
is a special case of the following very simple but quite general observation. Let
X
denote a
T
-variety equipped with a
T
-stable affine paving. Let
F\in D_{T}(X)
denote a
T
-equivariant complex whose underlying non-equivariant complex
\mathrm{For}^{T}(F)\in D(X)
is
!
-parity
(in the language of
[
22
]
, and with respect to the dimension pariversity
\diamond
). Then,
F
is equivariantly formal. Moreover, the property of being
!
-parity is preserved by
!
-restriction to any
T
-stable closed subvariety
i:Y\hookrightarrow X
. Thus, if
Y
also carries a
T
-stable affine paving, then
i^{!}F
is equivariantly formal.

Remark 2.1.3
.

Let
\mathrm{IC}_{\lambda}
be equipped with its natural structure of
T
-equivariant mixed Hodge module (the theory of equivariant mixed Hodge modules is not very well documented, but see
[
1
]
for a treatment) on
\mathrm{Gr}_{G}
, for which it is pure of weight 0. Note that
i_{I}^{!}\mathrm{IC}_{\lambda}
is not necessarily pure. Indeed,
i^{!}_{I}\mathrm{IC}_{\lambda}
is usually not a semisimple complex (which implies that it is not pure, by the BBD decomposition theorem). Therefore, the equivariant formality of Proposition
2.1.1
really requires the costalk parity considerations used in the proof and does not follow from the proof of
[
21
, Theorem 14.1(7)]
. On the other hand, the same argument works without change in settings which lack a theory of weights but in which strong parity vanishing results hold; for example, on the quaternionic affine Grassmannian of
[
12
]
.

Recall (see
[
28
, Section 3]
) that
\mathrm{Gr}_{G}
admits the Iwasawa decomposition

\mathrm{Gr}_{G}=\coprod_{\nu\in\Lambda}S_{\nu},

where
S_{\nu}=U(\mathcal{K})\cdot t^{\nu}
is the orbit of the group ind-scheme
U(\mathcal{K})
through the point
t^{\nu}\in\mathrm{Gr}_{G}
. Following
[
15
]
, we write
L^{-}B
for the ind-affine group ind-scheme representing the functor
R\mapsto L^{-}B(R)=B(R[t^{-1}])
and
L^{--}B\subseteq L^{-}B
for the kernel of the reduction morphism
L^{-}B\twoheadrightarrow B
given by
t^{-1}\mapsto 0
. Then, by the discussion in
[
3
]
surrounding Equation 1.2.6, there is a
T
-equivariant isomorphism

(2.1.4)

L^{--}B\simeq S_{\nu}

given on
\mathbb{C}
-points by
g\mapsto t^{\nu}gG(\mathcal{O})\in\mathrm{Gr}_{G}
. Here,
T
acts on
L^{--}B
acts by conjugation. In the proof of Proposition
2.1.5
, we write
V=V_{I}
for the unipotent radical of
P=P_{I}
to ease notation.

Proposition 2.1.5
.

For
x\in\mathrm{Gr}_{G}
, let
T_{x}\subseteq T
denote the stabilizer of
x
in
T
. Assume that
\alpha|_{T_{x}}:T_{x}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{m}
is a dominant morphism for every
\alpha\in\Phi^{+}_{G}\setminus\Phi_{L}^{+}
. Then,
x\in\mathrm{Gr}_{L}
.

Proof.

The point
x
belongs to the semi-infinite orbit
S_{\nu}
for a unique
\nu\in\Lambda
. We will use the
T
-equivariant isomorphism

L^{--}B\simeq S_{\nu}
of Equation
2.1.4
. Let
B^{\prime}=B\cap L
. We also have a
T
-equivariant isomorphism of schemes
V\times B^{\prime}\simeq B
(
(v,b^{\prime})\mapsto vb^{\prime}
), which induces a
T
-equivariant isomorphism
L^{--}V\times L^{--}B^{\prime}\simeq L^{--}B
. Since
B^{\prime}
is a Borel subgroup of
L
, we also obtain a
T
-equivariant isomorphism
L^{--}B^{\prime}\simeq S_{\nu}^{\prime}
, where
S_{\nu}^{\prime}
is the semi-infinite orbit in
\mathrm{Gr}_{L}
through
t^{\nu}
. Hence, there is a
T
-equivariant isomorphism
S_{\nu}\simeq L^{--}V\times S_{\nu}^{\prime}
. Let
\pi_{1}:S_{\nu}\rightarrow L^{--}V
denote the first projection. To show that
x\in\mathrm{Gr}_{L}
, it suffices to show that
\pi_{1}(x)
is the identity element
e\in L^{--}V
. Since
\pi_{1}
is
T
-equivariant, the subgroup
T_{x}\subseteq T
also fixes
\pi_{1}(x)
. Furthermore, we have a
T
-equivariant isomorphism of ind-affine ind-schemes

L^{--}V\simeq\prod_{\alpha\in\Phi^{+}_{G}\setminus\Phi^{+}_{L}}L^{--}U_{\alpha},

where
U_{\alpha}\subseteq U
is the root subgroup corresponding to the root
\alpha
. Let
\pi_{\alpha}
denote the projection onto the factor indexed by
\alpha
. We have an isomorphism
L^{--}U_{\alpha}\simeq L^{--}\mathbb{A}^{1}
under which the adjoint action of
T
on
L^{--}U_{\alpha}
is induced by the homomorphism
\alpha:T\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{m}
and the natural scaling action of
\mathbb{G}_{m}
on
L^{--}\mathbb{A}^{1}
. Since
\alpha|_{T_{x}}
dominates
\mathbb{G}_{m}
, we deduce that
(L^{--}\mathbb{A}^{1})^{T_{x}}=(L^{--}\mathbb{A}^{1})^{\mathbb{G}_{m}}=\{0\}
. Therefore,
\pi_{\alpha}(\pi_{1}(x))=\pi_{\alpha}(e)\in L^{--}U_{\alpha}
for each
\alpha\in\Phi^{+}_{G}\setminus\Phi^{+}_{L}
, which implies that
\pi_{1}(x)=e
, as claimed.
∎

We will now apply the localization theorem in
T
-equivariant cohomology. We again refer the reader to
[
21
]
for a general treatment as well as
[
35
, Theorem A.1.13]
for the specific statement that we use. Recall that we have a canonical graded ring isomorphism

H^{*}_{T}(\mathrm{pt},\mathbb{C})\simeq\operatorname{Sym}({\mathfrak{t}}^{*})

obtained (for example) by identifying
H_{T}^{*}(\mathrm{pt},\mathbb{C})
with the cohomology
H^{*}(BT,\mathbb{C})
of the (topological) classifying space of
T
. Here, the elements of
\mathfrak{t}^{*}
are placed in graded degree 2.

Let
i_{T}:\mathrm{Gr}_{T}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Gr}_{G}
denote the inclusion of the Grassmannian of
T
. We use the same symbol to denote the inclusion
\mathrm{Gr}_{T}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Gr}_{I}
.

Proposition 2.1.6
.

(a) Let

f_{I}=\prod_{\alpha\in\Phi^{+}_{G}\setminus\Phi^{+}_{L}}\alpha\in\operatorname{Sym}{\mathfrak{h}^{*}}=H_{T}^{*}(\mathrm{pt}).

The natural map

(2.1.7)

H^{*}_{T}(\mathrm{Gr}_{I},i_{I}^{!}\mathrm{IC}_{\lambda})\rightarrow H^{*}_{T}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G},\mathrm{IC}_{\lambda})

is an injective map of
H_{T}^{*}(\mathrm{pt},\mathbb{C})
-modules, and becomes an isomorphism after inverting
f_{I}
.

(b) Let

g_{I}=\prod_{\alpha\in\Phi_{L}^{+}}\alpha\in\operatorname{Sym}{\mathfrak{h}^{*}}=H_{T}^{*}(\mathrm{pt}).

The natural map

H_{T}^{*}(\mathrm{Gr}_{T},i_{T}^{!}\mathrm{IC}_{\lambda})\simeq H_{T}^{*}(\mathrm{Gr}_{T},i_{T}^{!}i_{I}^{!}\mathrm{IC}_{\lambda})\rightarrow H_{T}^{*}(\mathrm{Gr}_{I},i_{I}^{!}\mathrm{IC}_{\lambda})

is an injective map of
H_{T}^{*}(\mathrm{pt},\mathbb{C})
-modules, and becomes an isomorphism after inverting
g_{I}
.

Proof.

(a) Let
Z=\mathrm{Gr}^{\leq\lambda}_{G}
,
Z^{\prime}=Z\cap\mathrm{Gr}_{L}
, and
U=Z\setminus Z^{\prime}
. By Proposition
2.1.5
,
f_{I}
vanishes on
\mathrm{Lie}(T_{x})\subseteq\mathfrak{t}
for any point
x\in U\subseteq\mathrm{Gr}_{G}\setminus\mathrm{Gr}_{L}
(at least one of the roots
\alpha\in\Phi^{+}_{G}\setminus\Phi_{L}^{+}
is trivial on
T_{x}
since
x\not\in\mathrm{Gr}_{I}
). Hence, by the equivariant localization theorem (applied to the projective
T
-scheme
Z
, the
T
-stable closed subscheme
Z^{\prime}
, and the complex
\mathrm{IC}_{\lambda}\in D_{T}(Z)
), say in the form of
[
35
, Theorem A.1.13]
, the morphism
2.1.7
becomes an isomorphism after inverting
f_{I}
. By Proposition
2.1.1
,
H_{T}^{*}(\mathrm{Gr}_{I},i_{I}^{!}\mathrm{IC}_{\lambda})
is a free
H_{T}^{*}(\mathrm{pt},\mathbb{C})
-module, so the injectivity of the map
2.1.7
follows. The proof of (b) is similar, with the pair
(G,L)
replaced by
(L,T)
.
∎

Remark 2.1.8
.

Suppose that the Levi subgroup
L=L_{I}
is replaced by an arbitrary connected reductive subgroup
K\subseteq G
. Let
i:\mathrm{Gr}_{K}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Gr}_{G}
denote the induced closed immersion. If
K
contains a maximal torus
T
of
G
, then the arguments of this section carry over to show that the natural map

H_{T}^{*}(\mathrm{Gr}_{K},i^{!}\mathrm{IC}_{\lambda})\rightarrow H_{T}^{*}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G},\mathrm{IC}_{\lambda})

is injective and becomes an isomorphism after inverting a suitable element of
H_{T}^{*}(\mathrm{pt},\mathbb{C})
. However, without an analysis of the
T
-stabilizers of points
x\in\mathrm{Gr}_{G}\setminus\mathrm{Gr}_{K}
analogous to Proposition
2.1.5
, it is not clear
which
element of
H_{T}^{*}(\mathrm{pt},\mathbb{C})
must be inverted.

Remark 2.1.9
.

Proposition
2.1.6
can be understood in geometric terms as follows. The morphisms

H^{*}_{T}(\mathrm{Gr}_{T},i_{T}^{!}\mathrm{IC}_{\lambda})\rightarrow H_{T}^{*}(\mathrm{Gr}_{I},i_{I}^{!}\mathrm{IC}_{\lambda})\rightarrow H^{*}_{T}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G},\mathrm{IC}_{\lambda})

of free
H_{T}^{*}(\mathrm{pt},\mathbb{C})\simeq\operatorname{Sym}(\mathfrak{t}^{*})
-modules can be viewed as morphisms of (trivial) vector bundles over the affine space
\operatorname{Spec}{\operatorname{Sym}(\mathfrak{t}^{*})}=\mathfrak{t}
. Then, the second morphism restricts to an isomorphism
away from
the walls of the root system of
\check{G}
which are not walls of the root system of
\check{L}_{I}
. On the other hand, the first morphism restricts to an isomorphism away from the walls of the root system of
\check{L}_{I}
.

2.2.
Monoidal structure on corestriction

We will write
L=L_{I}
and
i=i_{I}
for now. We emphasize that the isomorphism of Theorem
1.5.2
is an isomorphism of
algebras
over the ring
R_{L}:=H_{L}^{*}(\mathrm{pt},\mathbb{C})
. However, our proof of Theorem
1.5.2
constructs this isomorphism of
R_{L}
-
modules
without any appeal to the existence of an algebra structure on the left hand side. The existence of a ring structure on
H^{*}_{L(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{L},i^{!}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{reg}})
appeared to us as an unexpected consequence of Theorem
1.5.2
, since the spectral side
\mathcal{O}(T^{*}(\check{G}/(\check{U},\psi_{I})))
is naturally a ring under the pointwise multiplication of regular functions.

The purpose of this section is therefore to equip
H^{*}_{L(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{L},i^{!}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{reg}})
with an
R_{L}
-algebra structure defined entirely on the geometric side. In the proof of Theorem
1.5.2
, we will show that our identification
H_{L(\mathcal{O})}^{*}(\mathrm{Gr}_{L},i^{!}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{reg}})\simeq\mathcal{O}(T^{*}(\check{G}/(\check{U},\psi_{I})))
is in fact an isomorphism of
R_{L}
-
algebras
.

We note that the existence of an algebra structure on
H^{*}_{L(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{L},i^{!}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{reg}})
is asserted in
[
11
, Section 5(xi)]
. However, we are unaware of a complete construction, so we will provide two in this section (and we will use both). The reader may wish to skim §§
2.6
to see how the results of this section fit together before reading further.

Remark 2.2.1
.

Consider the case
L=G
. The
R_{G}:=H_{G}^{*}(\mathrm{pt})
-module
H^{*}_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{reg}})
carries an evident
R_{G}
-algebra structure which was extensively exploited in
[
4
]
. It also plays a prominent role in the construction of Coulomb branches in
[
11
]
. Under the geometric Satake equivalence
\mathcal{P}_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})\simeq\operatorname{Rep}(\check{G})
,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{reg}}
corresponds to the left regular representation
\mathcal{O}(\check{G})
. The pointwise multiplication of regular functions equips
\mathcal{O}(\check{G})
with the structure of a
ring object
in the monoidal category
\operatorname{Rep}(\check{G})
. Passing back along geometric Satake to perverse sheaves, we find that
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{reg}}
carries a natural ring structure in the monoidal category
\mathcal{P}_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})
.

Recall that the fiber functor
H^{*}:\mathcal{P}_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})\rightarrow\mathrm{Mod}(\mathbb{C})
carries a natural monoidal structure
[
28
, Lemma 6.1]
. This construction of Mirković-Vilonen can be adapted (as in
[
34
, Section 2.3]
and
[
34
, Remark 2.5]
) to produce a monoidal structure on the equivariant cohomology functor
H^{*}_{G(\mathcal{O})}:\mathcal{P}_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})\rightarrow\operatorname{Mod}(R_{G})
. Since a monoidal functor takes ring objects to ring objects, we find a ring structure on
H_{G(\mathcal{O})}^{*}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G},\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{reg}})\in\operatorname{Mod}(R_{G})
.

Remark 2.2.2
.

We will generalize Remark
2.2.1
by equipping the object
i^{!}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{reg}}\in D_{L(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{L})
with a ring structure. However, we emphasize that the functor
i^{!}:D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})\rightarrow D_{L(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{L})
is
not
monoidal. For example, consider the case
L=T
. The functor

i_{0}^{!}:D_{T(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{T})\rightarrow\mathrm{Mod}(R_{T})

of costalk at the basepoint
t^{0}\in\mathrm{Gr}_{T}
is in fact monoidal. However, the composition
i_{0}^{!}\circ i^{!}\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{T(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{IC}_{0},-)
does not admit a monoidal structure: its restriction to
\mathcal{P}_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})\simeq\mathrm{Rep}(\check{G})
is naturally isomorphic to the functor
V\mapsto(V\otimes\mathrm{Sym}(\check{\mathfrak{g}}/\check{\mathfrak{u}}))^{\check{B}}
(by the main result of
[
20
]
, recalled above as Theorem
1.4.4
). In particular,
i^{!}
does not admit a monoidal structure.

Therefore, we will only aim to establish the weaker (but sufficient) result that
i^{!}
admits a natural
lax
monoidal structure. This will suffice: lax monoidal functors carry algebras to algebras, so
i^{!}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{reg}}
will acquire a ring structure in
D_{L(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{L})
.

Remark 2.2.3
.

It is surely possible to give a more elegant construction of the lax monoidal structure on
i^{!}
(even at the “cochain level”) by appealing to a suitable theory of sheaves and correspondences, such as that of
[
26
, Section A.5]
. However, as our Construction
2.2.14
makes essential use of non-invertible natural transformations (specifically, base change morphisms arising from non-Cartesian commutative squares), the kind of formalism described in
[
26
]
is not sufficient. It would be necessary to use a theory like that of
[
17
]
. However, as we do not wish to make our relatively pedestrian computations dependent on the (only partially documented) machinery of
(\infty,2)
-category theory, we will proceed directly.

Remark 2.2.4
.

In the remainder of this section, we will allow the Levi subgroup
L\subseteq G
to be replaced by an arbitrary connected reductive subgroup of
G
. However, our only use case in this paper is when
L
is a Levi subgroup.

Remark 2.2.5
.

Verdier duality defines an equivalence of categories

\mathbb{D}_{G}:D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})\simeq D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})^{\mathrm{op}}.

For objects
A,B\in D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})
, there is moreover a natural isomorphism

\mathbb{D}_{G}(A\star B)\simeq\mathbb{D}_{G}(A)\star\mathbb{D}_{G}(B).

See
[
2
, Lemma 7.2.8]
for the analogous fact about the convolution of
B
-equivariant sheaves on the flag variety
G/B
; the proof in our setting is identical. We also have a natural isomorphism
\mathbb{D}_{L}\circ i^{!}\simeq i^{*}\circ\mathbb{D}_{G}
. Therefore, the problem of equipping
i^{!}
with a lax monoidal structure is formally equivalent to that of equipping
i^{*}
with a
colax
monoidal structure (and that is what we will do).

Remark 2.2.6
.

In order to fix notation, we recall the definition of the convolution product on
D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})
(from
[
28
, Section 4]
), together with its associativity constraint (described briefly in
[
28
, Proposition 4.6]
). Let
\mathrm{Gr}_{G}^{(2)}=G(\mathcal{K})\times^{G(\mathcal{O})}\mathrm{Gr}_{G}=:\mathrm{Gr}_{G}\operatorname{\tilde{\times}}\mathrm{Gr}_{G}
denote the convolution Grassmannian. Let
m:\mathrm{Gr}_{G}^{(2)}\rightarrow\mathrm{Gr}_{G}
denote the multiplication map. We denote by
-\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}-:D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})\times D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})\rightarrow D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G}^{(2)})
the twisted external product.

Similarly, we let

\mathrm{Gr}_{G}^{(3)}=G(\mathcal{K})\times^{G(\mathcal{O})}G(\mathcal{K})\times^{G(\mathcal{O})}\mathrm{Gr}_{G}=:\mathrm{Gr}_{G}\operatorname{\tilde{\times}}\mathrm{Gr}_{G}\operatorname{\tilde{\times}}\mathrm{Gr}_{G}

denote the threefold convolution Grassmannian. Let
m^{23}:\mathrm{Gr}_{G}^{(3)}\rightarrow\mathrm{Gr}_{G}^{(2)}
denote the action map (given by
m^{23}(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3}G(\mathcal{O}))=(g_{1},g_{2}g_{3}G(\mathcal{O}))
), and let
m^{12}:\mathrm{Gr}_{G}^{(3)}\rightarrow\mathrm{Gr}_{G}^{(2)}
denote the multiplication of the first two factors (given by
m^{12}(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3}G(\mathcal{O}))=(g_{1}g_{2},g_{3}G(\mathcal{O}))
). We also have the twisted external product functors:

\displaystyle-\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}-

\displaystyle:D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G}^{(2)})\times D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})\rightarrow D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G}^{(3)})

\displaystyle-\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}-

\displaystyle:D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})\times D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G}^{(2)})\rightarrow D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G}^{(3)})

Finally, there is a triple multiplication map

m^{123}:\mathrm{Gr}_{G}^{(3)}\rightarrow\mathrm{Gr}_{G}

given by
m^{123}(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3}G(\mathcal{O}))=g_{1}g_{2}g_{3}G(\mathcal{O})
, and a triple external product

-\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}-\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}-:D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})\times D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})\times D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})\rightarrow D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G}^{(3)}).

The convolution product of
A,B\in D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})
is defined by

A\star B:=m_{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B).

The triple convolution product of
A,B,C\in D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})
is similarly defined by

A\star B\star C=m^{123}_{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}C).

The associativity constaint
\alpha_{A,B,C}
is a trifunctorial isomorphism

(2.2.7)

\alpha_{A,B,C}:(A\star B)\star C\simeq A\star(B\star C)

defined as a composition
\alpha_{A,B,C}=\gamma_{A,B,C}^{-1}\circ\beta_{A,B,C}
, where
\beta_{A,B,C}
is a natural isomorphism

(2.2.8)

\beta_{A,B,C}:(A\star B)\star C\simeq A\star B\star C

and
\gamma_{A,B,C}
is a natural isomorphism

\gamma_{A,B,C}:A\star(B\star C)\simeq A\star B\star C.

We spell out the definition of
\beta_{A,B,C}
(that of
\gamma_{A,B,C}
is analogous). We have
m^{123}=m\circ m^{12}
. Hence, we have a compositional isomorphism
m^{123}_{*}\simeq m_{*}\circ m_{*}^{12}
. It defines an isomorphism

\mathrm{comp}_{A,B,C}^{12}:m_{*}m_{*}^{12}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}C)\simeq m^{123}_{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}C)=A\star B\star C.

The next ingredient is the associativity constraint on the twisted external product

\delta_{A,B,C}:(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B)\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}C\simeq A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}C.

Applying
m^{12}_{*}
yields an isomorphism

m^{12}_{*}(\delta_{A,B,C}):m^{12}_{*}((A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B)\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}C)\simeq m_{*}^{12}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}C).

For any objects
E\in D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G}^{(2)})
,
F\in D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})
, we have an isomorphism

(2.2.9)

\epsilon_{E,F}:m_{*}E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}F\simeq m^{12}_{*}(E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}F).

By definition,
\epsilon_{E,F}
is obtained by adjunction from the composition of the evident natural maps

m^{12,*}(m_{*}E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}F)\xrightarrow{\xi^{12}_{E,F}}m^{*}m_{*}E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}F\xrightarrow{\eta_{E}\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}1}E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}F.

We may now take
E=A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B
and
F=C
to obtain the isomorphism

\epsilon_{A,B,C}:=\epsilon_{A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B,C}:m_{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B)\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}C\simeq m_{*}^{12}((A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B)\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}C).

Let

(2.2.10)

\sigma_{A,B,C}=m_{*}(m_{*}^{12}(\delta_{A,B,C})\circ\epsilon_{A,B,C}).

The isomorphism
\beta_{A,B,C}
is now defined to be the composition

\beta_{A,B,C}=\mathrm{comp}^{12}_{A,B,C}\circ\sigma_{A,B,C}.

When dealing with both groups
G
and
L
, we will generally use the same notation for the analogous maps and isomorphisms.

Remark 2.2.11
.

In addition to the morphism
i:\mathrm{Gr}_{L}\rightarrow\mathrm{Gr}_{G}
, we obtain morphisms
i_{2}:\mathrm{Gr}^{(2)}_{L}\rightarrow\mathrm{Gr}^{(2)}_{G}
and
i_{3}:\mathrm{Gr}^{(3)}_{L}\rightarrow\mathrm{Gr}^{(3)}_{G}
. We have the identities
m\circ i_{2}=i\circ m
,
m^{12}\circ i_{3}=i_{2}\circ m^{12}
,
m^{23}\circ i_{3}=i_{2}\circ m^{23}
, and
m^{123}\circ i_{3}=i\circ m^{123}
. We also have evident natural isomorphisms

(2.2.12)

\displaystyle\omega_{A,B}

\displaystyle:i_{2}^{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B)\simeq i^{*}A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}B

(2.2.13)

\displaystyle\omega_{A,B,C}

\displaystyle:i_{3}^{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}C)\simeq i^{*}A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}B\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}C.

Construction 2.2.14
.

We can now define the (non-unital part of the) colax monoidal structure on
i^{*}:D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})\rightarrow D_{L(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{L})
. For objects
A,B\in D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})
, we must define a bifunctorial map

\theta_{A,B}:i^{*}(A\star B)\rightarrow i^{*}A\star i^{*}B.

By definition,
A\star B=m_{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B)
. Since
m\circ i_{2}=i\circ m
, we deduce the existence of a base change morphism
\rho:i^{*}m_{*}\rightarrow m_{*}i_{2}^{*}
. Hence, we have a natural map

(2.2.15)

\rho_{A,B,C}:=\rho_{A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B}:i^{*}m_{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B)\rightarrow m_{*}i_{2}^{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B).

We also have the natural isomorphism
\omega_{A,B}:i_{2}^{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B)\simeq i^{*}A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}B
. Thus, we can define

(2.2.16)

\theta_{A,B}=m_{*}(\omega_{A,B})\circ\rho_{A,B,C}

The proof of the following proposition is the price that we pay for Remark
2.2.3
, i.e. our choice to construct
\theta_{A,B}
directly without the use of a higher categorical machinery that would presumably encode the commutativity of the diagrams below implicitly. The reader is encouraged to skip it on a first reading.

Proposition 2.2.17
.

The natural transformation
\theta:i^{*}(-\star-)\rightarrow i^{*}(-)\star i^{*}(-)
of functors
D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})\times D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})\rightarrow D_{L(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})
of Construction
2.2.14
is compatible with the associativity constraints of Remark
2.2.6
underlying the monoidal categories
D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})
and
D_{L(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{L})
. That is, for objects
A,B,C\in D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})
, the following diagram in
D_{L(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{L})
commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}((A\star B)\star C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}(A\star(B\star C))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}(A\star B)\star i^{*}C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}A\star i^{*}(B\star C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i^{*}A\star i^{*}B)\star i^{*}C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}A\star(i^{*}B\star i^{*}C)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}\alpha_{A,B,C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{A\star B,C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{A,B\star C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\theta}_{A,B}\star\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}\star\operatorname{\theta}_{B,C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{i^{*}A,i^{*}B,i^{*}C}}

Proof.

We expand the diagram horizontally, using the definition (
2.2.7
) of the associativity isomorphism
\alpha
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}((A\star B)\star C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}(A\star B\star C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}(A\star(B\star C))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}(A\star B)\star i^{*}C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}A\star i^{*}(B\star C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i^{*}A\star i^{*}B)\star i^{*}C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}A\star i^{*}B\star i^{*}C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}A\star(i^{*}B\star i^{*}C)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}\beta_{A,B,C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{A\star B,C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}\gamma_{A,B,C}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{A,B,C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{A,B\star C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\theta}_{A,B}\star\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}\star\operatorname{\theta}_{B,C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{i^{*}A,i^{*}B,i^{*}C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{i^{*}A,i^{*}B,i^{*}C}^{-1}}

It suffices to show that both of the small rectangles in this diagram commute. We will show that the one on the left commutes; the proof that the one on the right commutes is completely analogous. We now expand the diagram on the left, using the definitions (
2.2.16
) and (
2.2.8
) of
\theta
and
\beta
, respectively:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}m_{*}(m_{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B)\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}m_{*}m_{*}^{12}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}m^{123}_{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}i_{2}^{*}(m_{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B)\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}i_{2}^{*}m_{*}^{12}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}(i^{*}m_{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B)\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}m_{*}^{12}i_{3}^{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}^{123}i_{3}^{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}(m_{*}i_{2}^{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B)\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}(m_{*}(i^{*}A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}B)\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}m_{*}^{12}(i^{*}A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}B\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{123}_{*}(i^{*}A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}B\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}C)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}m_{*}\sigma_{A,B,C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{m_{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B)\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{comp}^{12}_{A,B,C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{m^{12}_{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}C)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho^{123}_{A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}\omega_{m_{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B),C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}i_{2}^{*}\sigma_{A,B,C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}\rho^{12,3}_{A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}(\rho_{A,B}\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{comp}^{12}_{i_{3}^{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}C)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}m_{*}^{12}\omega_{A,B,C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{123}_{*}\omega_{A,B,C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}(m_{*}\omega_{A,B}\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}\sigma_{i^{*}A,i^{*}B,i^{*}C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{comp}_{i^{*}A,i^{*}B,i^{*}C}^{12}}

Here, we have introduced the base change maps
\rho^{12,3}:i_{2}^{*}m_{*}^{12}\rightarrow m_{*}^{12}i_{3}^{*}
and
\rho^{123}:i^{*}m_{*}^{123}\rightarrow m_{*}^{123}i_{3}^{*}
deduced from the equalities
i_{2}m^{12}=m^{12}i_{3}
and
im^{123}=m^{123}i_{3}
, respectively. The upper left rectangle commutes by the naturality of the base change transformation
\rho:i^{*}m_{*}\rightarrow m_{*}i_{2}^{*}
of (
2.2.15
) applied to the isomorphism
\sigma_{A,B,C}
. The upper right rectangle commutes by the compatiblity of compositional isomorphisms with base change (see Proposition 2.11.7 of
[
2
]
and note that the Cartesian hypothesis is superfluous). The lower right rectangle commutes by the naturality of the compositional isomorphism
\mathrm{comp}^{12}:m_{*}m_{*}^{12}\simeq m_{*}^{123}
applied to the isomorphism
\omega_{A,B,C}
.

It therefore suffices to show that the lower left rectangle commutes. It is obtained by applying
m_{*}
to the the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{2}^{*}(m_{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B)\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{2}^{*}m_{*}^{12}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}m_{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B)\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}^{12}i_{3}^{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}i_{2}^{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B)\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}(i^{*}A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}B)\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}^{12}(i^{*}A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}B\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}C)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{m_{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B),C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{2}^{*}\sigma_{A,B,C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho^{12,3}_{A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{A,B}\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}^{12}\omega_{A,B,C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}\omega_{A,B}\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{i^{*}A,i^{*}B,i^{*}C}}

To show that the above diagram commutes, we expand it horizontally using the definition (
2.2.10
) of
\sigma
to obtain:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{2}^{*}(m_{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B)\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{2}^{*}m_{*}^{12}((A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B)\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{2}^{*}m_{*}^{12}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}m_{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B)\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\star)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}^{12}i_{3}^{*}((A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B)\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}^{12}i_{3}^{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}i_{2}^{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B)\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}^{12}(i_{2}^{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B)\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}(i^{*}A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}B)\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}^{12}((i^{*}A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}B)\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}^{12}(i^{*}A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}B\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}C)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{m_{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B),C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{2}^{*}\epsilon_{A,B,C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{2}^{*}m_{*}^{12}\delta_{A,B,C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho^{12,3}_{(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B)\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho^{12,3}_{A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{A,B}\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{12}_{*}i_{3}^{*}\delta_{A,B,C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}^{12}\omega_{A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B,C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}^{12}\omega_{A,B,C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}\omega_{A,B}\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{i_{2}^{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B),i^{*}C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}^{12}(\omega_{A,B}\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{i^{*}A,i^{*}B,i^{*}C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}^{12}\delta_{i^{*}A,i^{*}B,i^{*}C}}

The upper right rectangle commutes by the naturality of the base change map
\rho^{12,3}:i_{2}^{*}m_{*}^{12}\rightarrow m^{12}_{*}i^{*}_{3}
applied to the isomorphism
\delta_{A,B,C}
. The lower left rectangle commutes by the naturality of the isomorphism
\epsilon_{-,i^{*}C}:m_{*}(-)\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}C\simeq m_{*}^{12}(-\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}C)
of (
2.2.9
) applied to the isomorphism
\omega_{A,B}:i_{2}^{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B)\simeq i_{2}^{*}A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i_{2}^{*}B
of (
2.2.12
). The lower right rectangle commutes because it is obtained by applying the functor
m_{*}^{12}
to the commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{3}^{*}((A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B)\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{3}^{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{2}^{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B)\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i^{*}A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}B)\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}B\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{3}^{*}\delta_{A,B,C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B,C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{A,B,C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{A,B}\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{i^{*}A,i^{*}B,i^{*}C}}

It remains to show that the upper left rectangle
(\star)
commutes, which follows from the more general assertion that for
E\in D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G}^{(2)})
,
F\in D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})
, the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{2}^{*}(m_{*}E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{2}^{*}m_{*}^{12}(E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}m_{*}E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}^{12}i_{3}^{*}(E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}i_{2}^{*}E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}^{12}(i_{2}^{*}E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}F)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{m_{*}E,F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{2}^{*}\epsilon_{E,F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho^{12,3}_{E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{E}\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{12}_{*}\omega_{E,F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{i_{2}^{*}E,i^{*}F}}

By adjunction (and the definition (
2.2.9
) of
\epsilon
, which is used below to expand the bottom row horizontally), it suffices to show that the outer rectangle in the following diagram commutes (note that the middle vertical arrow
\mathrm{comp}
refers to the compositional isomorphism
m^{12,*}i_{2}^{*}\simeq i_{3}^{*}m^{12,*}
evaluated on
i_{2}^{*}m_{*}^{12}(E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}F)
):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{12,*}i_{2}^{*}(m_{*}E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{12,*}i_{2}^{*}m_{*}^{12}(E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{12,*}i_{2}^{*}m_{*}^{12}(E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{3}^{*}m^{12,*}m_{*}^{12}(E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{12,*}m^{12}_{*}i_{3}^{*}(E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{12,*}(i^{*}m_{*}E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{3}^{*}(E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{3}^{*}(E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{12,*}(m_{*}i_{2}^{*}E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{*}m_{*}i_{2}^{*}E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{2}^{*}E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{12,*}\omega_{m_{*}E,F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{12,*}i_{2}^{*}\epsilon_{E,F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{comp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{12,*}\rho^{12,3}_{E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{3}^{*}\eta^{12}_{E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta^{12}_{i_{3}^{*}(E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}F)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{12,*}(\rho^{12}_{E}\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{E,F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi^{12}_{m_{*}i_{2}^{*}E,i^{*}F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{i_{2}^{*}E}\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}1}

The upper right rectangle commutes by the very definition of the base change morphism
\rho^{12,3}_{E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}F}
. Thus, it suffices to show that the outer rectangle in the following diagram commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{12,*}i_{2}^{*}(m_{*}E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{12,*}i_{2}^{*}(m_{*}E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{12,*}i_{2}^{*}m_{*}^{12}(E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{3}^{*}m^{12,*}(m_{*}E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{3}^{*}m^{12,*}m_{*}^{12}(E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{12,*}(i^{*}m_{*}E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{3}^{*}(m^{*}m_{*}E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{3}^{*}(E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{2}^{*}m^{*}m_{*}E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{2}^{*}E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{12,*}(m_{*}i_{2}^{*}E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{*}m_{*}i_{2}^{*}E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{2}^{*}E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{12,*}\omega_{m_{*}E,F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{12,*}i_{2}^{*}\epsilon_{E,F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{comp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{comp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{3}^{*}\xi^{12}_{m_{*}E,F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{3}^{*}m^{12,*}\epsilon_{E,F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{3}^{*}\eta^{12}_{E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{12,*}(\rho^{12}_{E}\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{3}^{*}(\eta_{E}\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{m^{*}m_{*}E,F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{E,F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{2}^{*}\eta_{E}\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi^{12}_{m_{*}i_{2}^{*}E,i^{*}F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{i_{2}^{*}E}\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}1}

The upper right rectangle commutes by the naturality of the isomorphism
\mathrm{comp}:m^{12,*}i_{2}^{*}\simeq i_{3}^{*}m^{12,*}
. The rectangle below it commutes by (
i_{3}^{*}
applied to) the definition (
2.2.9
) of
\epsilon_{E,F}
. Hence, it suffices to show that the outer rectangle in the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{12,*}i_{2}^{*}(m_{*}E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{3}^{*}m^{12,*}(m_{*}E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\dagger)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{3}^{*}(m^{*}m_{*}E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{12,*}(i_{2}^{*}m_{*}E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{*}i_{2}^{*}m_{*}E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{2}^{*}m^{*}m_{*}E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{12,*}(m_{*}i_{2}^{*}E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{*}m_{*}i_{2}^{*}E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{2}^{*}E\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{12,*}\omega_{m_{*}E,F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{comp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{3}^{*}\xi^{12}_{m_{*}E,F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{m^{*}m_{*}E,F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{12,*}(\rho^{12}_{E}\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi^{12}_{i_{2}^{*}m_{*}E,i^{*}F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{comp}\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{*}\rho_{E}\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{2}^{*}\eta_{E}\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi^{12}_{m_{*}i_{2}^{*}E,i^{*}F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{i_{2}^{*}E}\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}1}

The lower left rectangle commutes by the naturality of the isomorphism
\xi^{12}:m^{12,*}(-\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}-)\simeq m^{*}(-)\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}-
applied to the map
\rho_{E}^{12}\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}1
. The lower right rectangle is obtained by applying the functor
-\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}F
to the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{*}i_{2}^{*}m_{*}E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{2}^{*}m^{*}m_{*}E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{*}m_{*}i_{2}^{*}E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{2}^{*}E.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{comp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{*}\rho^{12}_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{2}^{*}\eta_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{i_{2}^{*}E}}

This square commutes by the very definition of the base change map
\rho^{12}_{E}:i_{2}^{*}m_{*}E\rightarrow m_{*}i_{2}^{*}E
. We must verify the commutativity of the upper rectangle
(\dagger)
. The claim is that for
X,Y\in D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})
, the following diagram commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{12,*}i_{2}^{*}(X\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{3}^{*}m^{12,*}(X\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{3}^{*}(m^{*}X\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{12,*}(i_{2}^{*}X\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{*}i_{2}^{*}X\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{2}^{*}m^{*}X\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{12,*}\omega_{X,Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{comp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{3}^{*}\xi^{12}_{X,Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{m^{*}X,Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi^{12}_{i_{2}^{*}X,i^{*}Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{comp}\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}1}

Checking the commutativity of this diagram is left to the reader (reduce to verifying the commutativity of the corresponding diagram with untwisted external products, which follows from a straightforward compatiblity between the isomorphism
f^{*}(-\otimes-)\simeq f^{*}(-)\otimes f^{*}(-)
and the compositional isomorphisms
(gf)^{*}\simeq f^{*}g^{*}
).
∎

Next, we turn to the following problem. Suppose that
M\subseteq L
is a connected reductive subgroup of
L
(the reader should keep in the mind the case in which
M\subseteq L
is an inclusion of Levi subgroups of
G
; for instance, when
M=T
is a maximal torus). Let
j:\mathrm{Gr}_{M}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Gr}_{L}
denote the induced map on affine Grassmannians. We also have the composition
k=i\circ j:\mathrm{Gr}_{M}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Gr}_{G}
. In Construction
2.2.14
, we equipped the functors
i^{*}
,
j^{*}
, and
k^{*}
with (non-unital, for now) colax monoidal structures. On the other hand, we have a canonical isomorphism
k^{*}\simeq j^{*}\circ i^{*}
. We would like to show that this natural isomorphism is an isomorphism of (non-unital) colax monoidal functors.

Proposition 2.2.18
.

Let
j:\mathrm{Gr}_{M}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Gr}_{L}
,
i:\mathrm{Gr}_{L}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Gr}_{G}
, and
k=i\circ j:\mathrm{Gr}_{M}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Gr}_{G}
be as above. Equip the functors
i^{*}
,
j^{*}
, and
k^{*}
with the (non-unital) colax monoidal structures of Construction
2.2.14
. Then, the compositional isomorphism
k^{*}\simeq j^{*}\circ i^{*}
is an isomorphism of (non-unital) colax monoidal functors. More precisely, let
A,B\in D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})
. Then, the following diagram in
D_{M(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{M})
commutes:

(2.2.19)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}i^{*}(A\star B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}(i^{*}A\star i^{*}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}i^{*}A\star j^{*}i^{*}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{*}(A\star B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{*}A\star k^{*}B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{comp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}\theta_{A,B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{i^{*}A,i^{*}B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{comp}\star\operatorname{comp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{A,B}}

Proof.

We will notationally suppress all composition isomorphisms in the following argument (there are several that appear, but there is never ambiguity about which is meant). We can more explicitly express the above diagram (
2.2.19
) as follows.

(2.2.20)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}i^{*}m_{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}m_{*}(i^{*}A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}(j^{*}i^{*}A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}j^{*}i^{*}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{*}m_{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}(k^{*}A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}k^{*}B)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}\theta_{A,B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{i^{*}A,i^{*}B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{A,B}}

The following diagram commutes by the compatibility of composition with base change (Proposition 2.11.7 of
[
2
]
).

(2.2.21)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}i^{*}m_{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}m_{*}i_{2}^{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}j_{2}^{*}i_{2}^{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{*}m_{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}k_{2}^{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}\rho_{A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{i_{2}^{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B}}

The following diagram commutes because it is obtained by applying the functor
m_{*}
to the diagram which witnesses the compatibility of
\omega_{A,B}
with composition (see the last step in the proof of Proposition
2.2.17
).

(2.2.22)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}j_{2}^{*}i_{2}^{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}j_{2}^{*}(i^{*}A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}(j^{*}i^{*}A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}j^{*}i^{*}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}k_{2}^{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}(k^{*}A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}k^{*}B).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}\omega_{A,B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}\omega_{i^{*}A,i^{*}B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}\omega_{A,B}}

Pasting diagrams (
2.2.21
) and (
2.2.22
) together horizontally yields a commutative rectangle in which the left vertical, right vertical, and bottom horizontal arrows are equal to those of the original diagram (
2.2.20
). It therefore suffices to show that the top horizontal arrows coincide. Since the first and last morphisms in these four-fold compositions are equal, the claim amounts to verifying the commutativity of the following diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}m_{*}i_{2}^{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}m_{*}(i^{*}A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}j_{2}^{*}i_{2}^{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}j_{2}^{*}(i^{*}A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}B)}

This diagram commutes by the naturality of the base change map
j^{*}m_{*}\rightarrow m_{*}j_{2}^{*}
applied to the isomorphism
i_{2}^{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B)\simeq i^{*}A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i^{*}B
.
∎

Remark 2.2.23
.

Recall (for example, from
[
24
,
Tag 00CC
]
) that to give a monoidal structure on a category
\mathcal{C}
, it suffices to give an associative product
\star
on
\mathcal{C}
together with a
unit object
of
\mathcal{C}
(with respect to
\star
), that is, an object
1_{\mathcal{C}}\in\mathcal{C}
equipped with an isomorphism

1_{\mathcal{C}}\star 1_{\mathcal{C}}\simeq 1_{\mathcal{C}}.

We use this simplification below when discussing the unit constraint on the derived Satake category.

Remark 2.2.24
.

We recall the definition of the unit constraint on
(D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G}),\star)
(see
[
2
, Lemma 9.2.2]
). Let
\mathrm{IC}_{0}=\underline{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathrm{Gr}_{G}^{0}}
denote the denote
{G}(\mathcal{O})
-equivariant skyscraper at the basepoint
t^{0}\in\mathrm{Gr}_{G}
and let
j:\mathrm{pt}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Gr}_{G}
denote the closed inclusion of
t^{0}
. Let
j_{2}:\mathrm{pt}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Gr}_{G}\operatorname{\tilde{\times}}\mathrm{Gr}_{G}
denote the inclusion of the point
\mathrm{Gr}_{G}^{0}\operatorname{\tilde{\times}}\mathrm{Gr}_{G}^{0}
. Firstly, there is an isomorphism

\xi:\mathrm{IC}_{0}\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}\mathrm{IC}_{0}=j_{*}\underline{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathrm{pt}}\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}j_{*}\underline{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathrm{pt}}\simeq(j_{2})_{*}(\underline{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathrm{pt}}\boxtimes\underline{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathrm{pt}})\simeq(j_{2})_{*}\underline{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathrm{pt}}.

Since
m\circ j_{2}=j
, we have an isomorphism
(mj_{2})_{*}\simeq m_{*}(j_{2})_{*}
. Applying
m_{*}
to
\xi
therefore yields an isomorphism

\eta:\mathrm{IC}_{0}\star\mathrm{IC}_{0}\simeq m_{*}(j_{2})_{*}\underline{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathrm{pt}}\simeq j_{*}\underline{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathrm{pt}}=\mathrm{IC}_{0}.

The isomorphism
\eta
is the unit contraint.

Construction 2.2.25
.

Let
\mathrm{IC}_{0}^{\prime}\in D_{L(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{L})
denote the monoidal unit and
j^{\prime}:\mathrm{pt}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Gr}_{L}
the inclusion of the basepoint. We have an isomorphism
\chi:i^{*}\mathrm{IC}_{0}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{IC}_{0}^{\prime}
, which can be precisely defined as the composition

\chi:i^{*}\mathrm{IC}_{0}=i^{*}j_{*}\underline{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathrm{pt}}\simeq i^{*}i_{*}j^{\prime}_{*}\underline{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathrm{pt}}\xrightarrow{\sim}j^{\prime}_{*}\underline{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathrm{pt}}=\mathrm{IC}_{0}^{\prime}.

Proposition 2.2.26
.

The natural transformation
\theta:i^{*}(-\star-)\rightarrow i^{*}(-)\star i^{*}(-)
of functors
D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})\times D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})\rightarrow D_{L(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})
defined in Construction
2.2.14
is compatible with the unit constraints underlying the monoidal categories
D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})
and
D_{L(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{L})
. More precisely, the following diagram in
D_{L(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{L})
commutes:

(2.2.27)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}(\mathrm{IC}_{0}\star\mathrm{IC}_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}\mathrm{IC}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}\mathrm{IC}_{0}\star i^{*}\mathrm{IC}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{IC}^{\prime}_{0}\star\mathrm{IC}^{\prime}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{IC}_{0}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{\mathrm{IC}_{0},\mathrm{IC}_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi\star\chi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}

Proof.

It is at least obvious that all of the terms in (
2.2.27
) are isomorphic and supported at
t^{0}\in\mathrm{Gr}_{L}
. Hence, it suffices to prove the commutativity of the diagram after applying the functor
j^{*}
, where
j:\mathrm{pt}\rightarrow\mathrm{Gr}_{L}
denotes the inclusion of the basepoint. Let
k=ji:\mathrm{pt}\rightarrow\mathrm{Gr}_{G}
denote the composition. The diagram
j^{*}
(
2.2.27
) can be embedded into the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{*}(\mathrm{IC}_{0}\star\mathrm{IC}_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{*}(\mathrm{IC}_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}i^{*}(\mathrm{IC}_{0}\star\mathrm{IC}_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}i^{*}\mathrm{IC}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}(i^{*}\mathrm{IC}_{0}\star i^{*}\mathrm{IC}_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}(\mathrm{IC}^{\prime}_{0}\star\mathrm{IC}^{\prime}_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}\mathrm{IC}_{0}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}i^{*}\mathrm{IC}_{0}\star j^{*}i^{*}\mathrm{IC}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}\mathrm{IC}_{0}^{\prime}\star j^{*}\mathrm{IC}_{0}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathrm{pt}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{*}\mathrm{IC}_{0}\star k^{*}\mathrm{IC}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathrm{pt}}\star\underline{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathrm{pt}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathrm{pt}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{*}\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{comp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{comp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}i^{*}\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}\theta_{\mathrm{IC}_{0},\mathrm{IC}_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}\chi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}(\chi\,\star\,\chi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{i^{*}\mathrm{IC}_{0},i^{*}\mathrm{IC}_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{\mathrm{IC}^{\prime}_{0},\mathrm{IC}^{\prime}_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(j^{*}\chi)\,\star\,(j^{*}\chi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{comp}\star\operatorname{comp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi^{\prime}\star\chi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi^{\prime\prime}\,\star\,\chi^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\otimes 1\mapsto 1}

Here, the isomorphism
\chi^{\prime}:j^{*}\mathrm{IC}_{0}^{\prime}\rightarrow\underline{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathrm{pt}}
(respectively,
\chi^{\prime\prime}:k^{*}\mathrm{IC}_{0}\underline{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathrm{pt}}
) is obtained from Construction
2.2.25
after replacing the pair
L\subseteq G
by the pair
\mathrm{pt}\subseteq L
(respectively,
\mathrm{pt}\subseteq G
). Moreover, the unlabelled map
j^{*}\mathrm{IC}^{\prime}_{0}\star j^{*}\mathrm{IC}_{0}^{\prime}\rightarrow\underline{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathrm{pt}}
is defined as the composition
j^{*}\mathrm{IC}_{0}^{\prime}\star j^{*}\mathrm{IC}_{0}^{\prime}\rightarrow\underline{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathrm{pt}}\star\underline{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathrm{pt}}\simeq\underline{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathrm{pt}}
(and is included only for formatting purposes).

The commutativity of the top rectangle follows from the naturality of the isomorphism
\mathrm{comp}:j^{*}i^{*}\simeq k^{*}
applied to the morphism
\eta
. The commutativity of the upper left small rectangle follows from the naturality of the morphism
\theta:j^{*}(-\star-)\rightarrow j^{*}(-)\star j^{*}(-)
applied to
\chi\star\chi
. The commutativity of the upper right small rectangle will follow from the case
L=\mathrm{pt}
. The commutativity of the lower right small rectangle is trivial. The lower left small rectangle commutes by the compatiblity of
\chi
with composition. The commutativity of the large outer rectangle will follow from the case
L=\mathrm{pt}
. These observations imply the commutativity of the second rectangle from the top (that is, the diagram
j^{*}
(
2.2.27
)).

We may therefore assume that
L=\mathrm{pt}
is the trivial subgroup. In this case, the claim is obvious.
∎

Corollary 2.2.28
.

(1)

The constructions of
2.2.14
and
2.2.25
equip the functor
i^{*}:D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})\rightarrow D_{L(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{L})
with a colax monoidal structure.

(2)

The constructions of
2.2.5
,
2.2.14
, and
2.2.25
equip the functor
i^{!}:D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})\rightarrow D_{L(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{L})
with a lax monoidal structure.

2.3.
Fusion

In this section, we will give an alternative description of our lax monoidal structure on
i^{!}
. We recall some definitions and constructions from
[
16
, Section 2.1.2]
, to which we refer the reader for more details. Let
\mathrm{Aut}
denote the automorphism group scheme of the
\mathbb{C}
-algebra
\mathcal{O}
. It is a pro-algebraic group and acts naturally on the affine Grassmannian
\mathrm{Gr}_{G}
by loop rotation. Let
X=\mathbb{A}^{1}
and let
\tilde{X}\rightarrow X
denote the canonical
\mathrm{Aut}
-torsor over
X
(whose fiber over
x\in X
is the
\mathrm{Aut}
-torsor parameterizing uniformizers of the completed local ring
\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X,x}
). Recall that the
Beilinson-Drinfeld Grassmannian
is the ind-scheme over
X
given by the twisted product

\mathrm{Gr}_{G,X}=\tilde{X}\times^{\mathrm{Aut}}\mathrm{Gr}_{G}\rightarrow X.

For any affine
X
-scheme
x:S\rightarrow X
, the set
\mathrm{Gr}_{G,X}(S)
can be identified canonically with the set of isomorphism classes of pairs
(\mathcal{P},\sigma)
, where
\mathcal{P}
is a
G
-torsor over
X_{S}=X\times S
and
\sigma
is a trivialization of
\mathcal{P}
over the subscheme
X_{S}\setminus\Gamma_{x}
, the complement of the graph
\Gamma_{x}:S\rightarrow X_{S}
(Lemma 3 of
[
16
, Section 2.1.2]
). The group scheme
\mathrm{Aut}
acts through group automorphisms on the arc group
G(\mathcal{O})
. Therefore, we can form the group scheme
\mathcal{G}_{X}
over
X
given by

\mathcal{G}_{X}:=\tilde{X}\times^{\mathrm{Aut}}G(\mathcal{O})\rightarrow X.

Since the action
G(\mathcal{O})\times\mathrm{Gr}_{G}\rightarrow\mathrm{Gr}_{G}
is
\mathrm{Aut}
-equivariant, we obtain an action of the group
X
-scheme
\mathcal{G}_{X}
on the
X
-ind-scheme
\mathrm{Gr}_{G,X}
. The twisted external product defines a functor

p_{2}^{\dagger}:=\underline{\mathbb{C}}_{\tilde{X}}[1]\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}-:D_{G(\mathcal{O})\rtimes\mathrm{Aut}}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})\rightarrow D_{\mathcal{G}_{X}}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G,X}).

See
[
3
, Section 2.4.1]
for a brief discussion of the definition of the above
\mathcal{G}_{X}
-equivariant derived category, and
[
3
, Chapter 10]
for a thorough treatment. On the other hand, we have Gaitsgory’s degeneration
\pi:\mathrm{Gr}_{G,X}^{\prime}\rightarrow X
from
[
16
, Section 3.1.1]
. It is the ind-scheme over
X
whose points over an affine
X
-scheme
x:S\rightarrow X
are given by isomorphism classes of pairs
(\mathcal{P},\sigma)
, where
\mathcal{P}
is a
G
-torsor over
X_{S}
and
\sigma
is a trivialization of
\mathcal{P}
over the subscheme
X_{S}\setminus(\Gamma_{x}\cup\Gamma_{0})
, where
\Gamma_{0}
denotes the graph of the map
S\rightarrow\operatorname{Spec}{\mathbb{C}}\xrightarrow{0}\mathbb{A}^{1}=X
. Note that
\mathcal{G}_{X}
acts on
\mathrm{Gr}_{G,X}^{\prime}
through modification of the trivialization
\sigma
. There are canonical isomorphisms

\displaystyle\mathrm{Gr}_{G,0}^{\prime}:=\mathrm{Gr}_{G,X}^{\prime}|_{\{0\}}\simeq\mathrm{Gr}_{G}

\displaystyle\mathrm{Gr}^{\prime}_{G,X-0}:=\mathrm{Gr}_{G,X}^{\prime}|_{X\setminus\{0\}}\simeq\mathrm{Gr}_{G}\times\mathrm{Gr}_{G,X}|_{X\setminus\{0\}}=\mathrm{Gr}_{G}\times\mathrm{Gr}_{G,X-0}.

Hence, we obtain a functor

-\boxtimes p_{2}^{\dagger}(-)|_{\mathrm{Gr}_{G,X-0}}:D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})\times D_{G(\mathcal{O})\rtimes\mathrm{Aut}}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})\rightarrow D_{\mathcal{G}_{X-0}}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G,X-0}^{\prime}).

Here, we write
\mathcal{G}_{X-0}
for the restriction of
\mathcal{G}_{X}
to the open subscheme
X\setminus\{0\}
. Finally, let
\Psi:D_{\mathcal{G}_{X-0}}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G,X}^{\prime})\rightarrow D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})
denote the functor of nearby cycles in the family
\pi
(we are implicitly using the canonical identification of group schemes
\mathcal{G}_{X}|_{\{0\}}\simeq G(\mathcal{O})
provided by the uniformizer
t\in\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X,0}=\mathbb{C}[[t]]
here). Following Gaitsgory, we define

(2.3.1)

C(A,B):=\Psi(A\boxtimes p_{2}^{\dagger}B|_{\mathrm{Gr}_{G,X-0}}).

Because the forgetful functor
D_{G(\mathcal{O})\rtimes\mathrm{Aut}}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})\rightarrow D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})
is an equivalence of categories (it is fully faithful because
\mathrm{Aut}
is pro-unipotent, and essentially surjective because its image contains
\mathcal{P}_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})
by
[
29
, Proposition 3.2.2]
which generates
D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})
as a triangulated category), we may regard
C(-,-)
as a bifunctor

C(-,-):D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})\times D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})\rightarrow D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G}).

As usual, we will use the same notation for the corresponding objects defined using the subgroup
L
. We have closed immersions

i_{X}:\mathrm{Gr}_{L,X}=\tilde{X}\times^{\mathrm{Aut}}\mathrm{Gr}_{L}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}_{X}\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}i}\tilde{X}\times^{\mathrm{Aut}}\mathrm{Gr}_{G}=\mathrm{Gr}_{G,X}

i^{\prime}_{X}:\mathrm{Gr}^{\prime}_{L,X}\rightarrow\mathrm{Gr}_{G,X}^{\prime}

globalizing the
\mathrm{Aut}
-equivariant closed immersion
i:\mathrm{Gr}_{L}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Gr}_{G}
. The morphism
i_{X}^{\prime}
takes a point
(\mathcal{P},\sigma)
of
\mathrm{Gr}_{L,X}^{\prime}
over an affine
X
-scheme
x:S\rightarrow X
to the point
(\mathcal{P}^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})\in\mathrm{Gr}_{G,X}^{\prime}(S)
given by the induced
G
-torsor
\mathcal{P^{\prime}}=\mathcal{P}\times^{L}G
and the trivialization
\sigma^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
over
X_{S}\setminus(\Gamma_{x}\cup\Gamma_{0})
induced by
\sigma
. Let
i_{X-0}
,
i^{\prime}_{X-0}
denote their restrictions to
X\setminus\{0\}
.

Construction 2.3.2
.

Let
A,B\in D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})
. We will define a natural map

\Theta_{A,B}:i^{*}C(A,B)\rightarrow C(i^{*}A,i^{*}B)

in
D_{L(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})
. We have a natural isomorphism (witnessing the monoidality of
*
-pullback with respect to the tensor product)

(2.3.3)

\Omega_{A,B}:(i_{X-0}^{\prime})^{*}(A\boxtimes p_{2}^{\dagger}B|_{\mathrm{Gr}_{G,X-0}})\xrightarrow{\Omega_{1}}i^{*}A\boxtimes i^{*}p_{2}^{\dagger}B|_{\mathrm{Gr}_{G,X-0}}\xrightarrow{1\,\boxtimes\,\Omega_{2}}i^{*}A\boxtimes p_{2}^{\dagger}(i^{*}B)|_{\mathrm{Gr}_{G,X-0}}.

We have also used the compositional isomorphism
i_{2}^{*}p_{2}^{*}\simeq p_{2}^{*}i^{*}
to commute
i^{*}
with
p_{2}^{\dagger}
. There is also a natural transformation
[
2
, Lemma 4.4.8]

(2.3.4)

\zeta:i^{*}\circ\Psi\rightarrow\Psi\circ(i_{X-0}^{\prime})^{*}.

Applying
\Psi
to the isomorphism
\Omega_{A,B}
and then precomposing with
\zeta
defines the natural transformation
\Theta_{A,B}
. That is,

\Theta_{A,B}=\Psi(\Omega_{A,B})\circ\zeta_{A\,\boxtimes\,p_{2}^{\dagger}B|_{\mathrm{Gr}_{G,X-0}}}.

The following fundamental result of Gaitsgory relates the bifunctor
C(-,-)
to the convolution product.

Theorem 2.3.5

(Gaitsgory
[
16
, Proposition 6]
)
.

Let
A,B\in D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})
. There is a natural isomorphism in
D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})

\varpi_{A,B}:C(A,B)\simeq A\star B.

Remark 2.3.6
.

Gaitsgory’s result only requires
G(\mathcal{O})
-equivariance on one of the factors; the other need only belong to the constructible derived category
D(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})
(with the obvious caveat that
\varpi_{A,B}
is in that case only an isomorphism of non-equivariant complexes). However, we will only need the version stated above.

Construction 2.3.7
.

We need to unpack the construction of the isomorphism
\varpi_{A,B}
from Gaitsgory’s proof of
[
16
, Proposition 6]
. Let
\widetilde{\mathrm{Gr}}^{\prime}_{G,X}\rightarrow X
denote the ind-scheme over
X
whose set of points
\widetilde{\mathrm{Gr}}^{\prime}_{G,X}(S)
over an affine
X
-scheme
x:S\rightarrow X
is the set of isomorphism classes of tuples
(\mathcal{P}_{0},\mathcal{P}_{1},\eta_{0},\eta_{1})
, where
\mathcal{P}_{0}
and
\mathcal{P}_{1}
are
G
-torsors over
X_{S}
,
\eta_{0}
is an isomorphism of
G
-torsors
\eta_{0}:\mathcal{P}_{0}|_{X_{S}\setminus\Gamma_{x}}\simeq\mathcal{P}_{1}|_{X_{S}\setminus\Gamma_{x}}
, and
\eta_{1}
is a trivialization of the
G
-torsor
\mathcal{P}_{1}
over
X_{S}\setminus\Gamma_{0}
. We have a map
p:\widetilde{\mathrm{Gr}}_{G,X}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathrm{Gr}_{G,X}
of
X
-ind-schemes taking an
S
-point
(\mathcal{P}_{0},\mathcal{P}_{1},\eta_{0},\eta_{1})\in\widetilde{\mathrm{Gr}}^{\prime}_{G,X}(S)
to the
S
-point
(\mathcal{P}_{1},\eta_{1})\in\mathrm{Gr}_{G,X}(S)
and a map
\mu:\widetilde{\mathrm{Gr}}_{G,X}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathrm{Gr}_{G,X}^{\prime}
taking an
S
-point
(\mathcal{P}_{0},\mathcal{P}_{1},\eta_{0},\eta_{1})\in\widetilde{\mathrm{Gr}}^{\prime}_{G,X}(S)
to the
S
-point
(\mathcal{P}_{0},\eta_{0}\circ\eta_{1}|_{X_{S}\setminus(\Gamma_{x}\cup\Gamma_{0})})\in{\mathrm{Gr}}^{\prime}_{G,X}(S)
.

On the other hand, consider the
G(\mathcal{O})
-torsor
\widetilde{\mathrm{Gr}}_{G,X}\rightarrow\mathrm{Gr}_{G,X}
, defined by the fact that for any affine
X
-scheme
x:S\rightarrow X
,
\widetilde{\mathrm{Gr}}_{G,X}(S)
is the set of isomorphism classes of tuples
(\mathcal{P},\eta,\gamma)
, where
(\mathcal{P},\eta)
is an
S
-point of
\mathrm{Gr}_{G,X}
and
\gamma
is a trivialization of
\mathcal{P}
over the formal neighborhood
\widehat{\Gamma}_{0}
of
\Gamma_{0}\subseteq X_{S}
. Then, there is an isomorphism

\widetilde{\mathrm{Gr}}_{G,X}^{\prime}\simeq\mathrm{Gr}_{G}\times^{G(\mathcal{O})}\widetilde{\mathrm{Gr}}_{G,X}=:\mathrm{Gr}_{G}\operatorname{\tilde{\times}}\mathrm{Gr}_{G,X}

of ind-schemes over
X
, under which
p
corresponds to the natural projection to the second factor. This description allows us to consider the twisted external product

-\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}-:D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})\times D_{\mathcal{G}_{X}}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G,X})\rightarrow D_{\mathcal{G}_{X}}(\widetilde{\mathrm{Gr}}_{G,X}^{\prime}).

Let
A,B\in D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})
. Form the complex
A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}p_{2}^{\dagger}B\in D_{\mathcal{G}_{X}}(\widetilde{\mathrm{Gr}}^{\prime}_{G,X})
. Then, there is a natural isomorphism
[
16
, Lemma 5(b)]

(2.3.8)

\mu_{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}p_{2}^{\dagger}B)|_{\mathrm{Gr}_{G,X-0}}\simeq A\boxtimes(p_{2}^{\dagger}B|_{\mathrm{Gr}_{G,X-0}})

induced by the projection formula. Let
\mu_{0}
denote the fiber of
\mu
over
0\in X
. There is a natural transformation
[
2
, Lemma 4.8.8]

(2.3.9)

\Psi\mu_{*}\rightarrow\mu_{0*}\Psi,

which is an isomorphism by the ind-properness of
\mu
(note that the nearby cycles functor
\Psi
appearing in the domain of this natural transformation is that corresponding to the family
\mathrm{Gr}_{G,X}^{\prime}\rightarrow X
whereas that in the domain is nearby cycles in the family
\widetilde{\mathrm{Gr}}_{G,X}^{\prime}\rightarrow X
). Combining it with (
2.3.8
) yields an isomorphism

\mu_{0*}\Psi(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}p_{2}^{\dagger}B)\simeq C(A,B).

Since
\widetilde{\mathrm{Gr}}_{G,X}^{\prime}\rightarrow X
can be trivialized by the choice of a global coordinate
t
on
\mathbb{A}^{1}
, we also have a natural isomorphism

(2.3.10)

\Psi(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}p_{2}^{\dagger}B)\simeq(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}p_{2}^{\dagger}B)|_{\mathrm{Gr}_{G}^{(2)}}\simeq A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B,

where
\mathrm{Gr}_{G}^{(2)}
is identified with with the fiber of
\widetilde{\mathrm{Gr}}_{G,X}^{\prime}\rightarrow X
over
0\in X
. Since
\mu_{0}=m
is the convolution map, we obtain the desired isomorphism

\varpi_{A,B}:A\star B=m_{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}B)\simeq\mu_{0*}\Psi(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}p_{2}^{\dagger}B)\simeq C(A,B).

Construction 2.3.11
.

We can define a morphism

\tilde{i}^{\prime}_{X}:\widetilde{\mathrm{Gr}}_{L,X}^{\prime}\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathrm{Gr}}^{\prime}_{G,X}

by sending an
S
-point (for
x:S\rightarrow X
an affine
X
-scheme) of
\widetilde{\mathrm{Gr}}_{L,X}^{\prime}
given by a tuple
(\mathcal{P}_{0},\mathcal{P}_{1},\eta_{0},\eta_{1})
to the
S
-point of
\widetilde{\mathrm{Gr}}_{G,X}^{\prime}
given by
(\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{0},\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{1},\eta^{\prime}_{0},\eta^{\prime}_{1})
, where
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{i}=\mathcal{P}_{i}\times^{L}G
is the
G
-torsor associated to the
L
-torsor
\mathcal{P}_{i}
,
\eta_{0}^{\prime}
is the isomorphism
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{0}|_{X_{S}\setminus\Gamma_{x}}\simeq\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{1}|_{X_{S}\setminus\Gamma_{x}}
induced from
\eta_{0}^{\prime}
, and
\eta_{1}^{\prime}
is the trivialization of
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{1}
away from
\Gamma_{0}
induced from the trivialization
\eta_{1}
of
\mathcal{P}_{1}
away from
\Gamma_{0}
. Given
A,B\in D_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})
, we have a natural isomorphism

(2.3.12)

\widetilde{\Omega}_{A,B}:(\tilde{i}^{\prime}_{X})^{*}(A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}p_{2}^{\dagger}B)\simeq i^{*}A\operatorname{\widetilde{\boxtimes}}p_{2}^{\dagger}i^{*}B.

Note that the fiber of
\tilde{i}_{X}^{\prime}
over
0\in X
identifies with the map
i_{2}:\mathrm{Gr}_{L}^{(2)}\rightarrow\mathrm{Gr}_{G}^{(2)}
. Hence, the construction of (
2.3.4
) defines a natural transformation

\zeta_{2}:i_{2}^{*}\Psi\rightarrow\Psi(\tilde{i}_{X}^{\prime})^{*}.

We can now formulate the global interpretation of our lax monoidal structure on
i^{!}
(equivalently, of the colax monoidal structure on
i^{*}
).

Proposition 2.3.13
.

Let
